PREFACE 

This series of textbooks is the outgrowth of a number of years' work in teaching in the public schools, supervising public-school teachers, supervising practice teaching in training schools, and teaching courses in methods of teaching arithmetic.

The effort to prepare a series of texts that would develop better skills in arithmetic resulted in the following features, some of which are distinctive: 

1. The setting out at the beginning of each grade of the objectives for which that grade should be responsible. 

2. The statement of specific objectives for each unit of new subject matter. 

3. The inclusion of tests at the beginning of each grade, covering the essential elements that should have been mastered in previous grades. 

4. The inclusion at frequent intervals of tests that enable the teacher to check the efficiency of the pupils. 

5. The inclusion of time and accuracy standards for drill material. 

6. A comprehensive examination at the end of the work for each grade, which enables the teacher to devote remedial efforts to those pupils who are deficient. 

7. The extraordinary care with which the material has been graded as to difficulty. 

8. The inclusion of exercises for the maintenance of skills, spaced so as to care for this responsibility. 3

PREFACE 

Much of the material included in this series of texts was tried out with beginning teachers in training. Modifications, additions, and eliminations were made in the light of their experience in teaching it. 

Thanks are due to Miss Elizabeth McKinney, Miss 

Bertha Byrns, Miss Rosa Lee Hammond, and Mr. James

H. Zant, critic teachers in the Training School at Southeastern Teachers College, Durant, Oklahoma, for coöperation in testing out the materials and for criticisms, suggestions, and encouragement.

GENERAL SUGGESTIONS TO THE TEACHER 

Adherence to the following suggestions will insure the most effective use of this series of books. 

1. Objectives. Get in mind the objectives set out at the beginning of each grade. These are the goals toward which everything in the text leads. 

2. Mastery of Each New Ability. The general mas tery of arithmetic consists in the mastery of a large number of specific skills, the mastery of a fairly definite body of knowledge, and the mastery of a number of more or less general abilities. This book contains the material for accomplishing these ends, arranged in carefully graded steps. If each step is mastered before the next one is taken up, the learning process will move along in a satisfactory way. Most of the subject matter of arithmetic is definitely related, and where the material is properly graded, the mastery of one step leads directly to the mastery of the step that follows. Remember, mastery is much more important than speed. 

3. Maintaining Skills. No matter how thoroughly skills may have been built up, they will deteriorate unless provision is made for their maintenance. You are urged, therefore, to treat seriously the exercises, placed throughout the text, designed to maintain these skills. 

4. Attainment Tests. There is but one way to determine how effective and permanent your teaching is and that is by testing. At frequent intervals material has 11

GENERAL SUGGESTIONS TO THE TEACHER 

been included for this purpose. If the pupils show weaknesses, analyze the tests, determine definitely what the weakness is, and provide a proper review. Much of this review should be accomplished by individual help and individual assignments. 

5. Standards. Throughout the series standards are set for the fundamental processes. Their purpose is threefold: (1) They give the teacher a measure of her class, so that she can tell when a specific ability is sufficiently well mastered to warrant passing on to the next difficulty. (2) They force the pupils to automatize the facts and processes that should be made automatic, and thus prevent the building up of counting and other cumbersome habits. (3) They furnish a motive for drill work by setting a goal for which each pupil may strive. They also stimulate the spirit of competition between individuals and between groups, as well as the ambition of each pupil to improve his own previous record. 

6. Finding the Median. To determine whether the class is up to the standard, find the average score, or the median (middle) score. If the class score is equal to, or larger than, the standard score, it is up to standard. 

Pupils below the median should receive individual help. 

An easy and sufficiently accurate way to find the median is as follows: (1) Arrange or stack the papers according to the size of the scores, starting with the smallest score and building up to the largest. (2) Count from either end to the middle paper, the score of which is the median score for the class.

GENERAL SUGGESTIONS TO THE TEACHER 13 

For example, if there are 15 pupils in the class, the eighth paper is the middle one and the score on this is the median. If there are 22 pupils in the class, the middle score is halfway between the scores on the eleventh and twelfth papers. If the score on the eleventh paper is 

14 and the score on the twelfth paper is 16, the median is 15. If the score on the eleventh paper is 5 and the score on the twelfth paper is 6, the median is 5. 

7. Final Attainment Tests. At the end of the work of each grade there has been placed a comprehensive examination covering all the objectives for that grade. 

This is designed as a final check on the work of the year. 

It should be given a sufficient length of time before the close of the school year so that remedial work may be provided for those pupils who need it. 

8. Tests on Preceding Work. The knowledge and skills which the Tests on Preceding Work measure are those which it is necessary for the pupils to have before proceeding with the work of the next grade. The time to be spent on these tests and the needed reviews will vary from one week to six weeks. These tests may be used also to assist in the proper placement of new pupils received from other schools throughout the year. Do not give all the tests before starting remedial work. 

If any considerable number of the class is shown by a particular test to be weak, stop and spend as many lessons as are needed to correct the weakness. If only a few pupils are weak, take care of them by making individual assignments and by giving individual help. 

The tests should be conducted in an informal way, after the manner of a recitation. The purpose is not

GENERAL SUGGESTIONS TO THE TEACHER 

to accumulate individual records, but to discover individual weaknesses in a group of pupils, to the end that they may be recognized and corrected before setting out to accomplish the objectives of the new grade. 

For the portions of the test that are to be written on paper, only the answers should be written. This may be done by having the pupils hold the edge of a sheet of paper directly under a row of problems and write the answers on the paper under each problem. This timesaving device should be used a great deal for written work throughout the year. When more than one row of problems is to be solved, the paper should be folded down and back after a row of answers has been written, and the fold should be held under the next row of problems. The following illustration shows how the paper should look when unfolded. 1. 14 17 13 12 15 19 2. 16 11 10 14 12 11 3. 18 13 10 16 19 15 4. 17 12 18 10 13 11

THIRD GRADE OBJECTIVES FOR THE THIRD GRADE 1. Reading and Writing Numbers 

To read and write Arabic numbers to 10,000.

To read and write Roman numerals through 50. 

2. Fundamental Processes 

To give automatic responses to the primary addition facts.

To add single columns extended to six addends.

To add multiple columns extended to five addends three digits wide.

To give automatic responses to the primary subtraction facts.

To subtract problems extended to numbers four digits wide.

To give automatic responses to the primary multiplication facts.

To solve short multiplication problems extended to four-digit multiplicands, with one-digit multipliers.

To give automatic responses to the primary division facts. 15

THIRD GRADE 

To solve short division extended to four-digit dividends, with one-digit divisors, the remainders being left as integers. 

3. United States Money 

To read and write dollars and cents to $10,000. 

To apply the four fundamental processes, no numbers in the problems being more than four digits wide. 

4. Denominate Numbers

To recognize and use such familiar measures as inch, yard, quart, gallon, day, week.

To know the abbreviations for these units of measure.

To use these units of measure in concrete problems. 

5. Fractions 

To understand what a fraction is.

To find a fractional part of a number, when the numerator of the fraction is 1 and the denominator is any digit. 

6. Practical Problems

To read understandingly and to solve simple one-step problems involving the four fundamental processes.

THIRD GRADE EVEN AND ODD NUMBERS A CIRCUS PARADE 

66 In the circus parade, the animals marched by 2's. 

When the elephants went past, Jack said, 'Oh, there are 2, 4, 6, 8, 10 elephants." He counted the people and the animals in the parade in the same way. 

1. Count by 2's to 50, beginning with 2.

All the numbers named when you count by 2's, starting with 2, are called even numbers. As all the even numbers end with 0, 2, 4, 6, or 8, you can tell whether any number is an even number just by looking at the last figure on the right. 

576 is an even number because the last number on the right is 6. 

2. Which of these numbers are even? Why? 

140 141 292 457 

1000 74 75

PRIMARY ADDITION FACTS 

23. 

3. Count by 2's to 50, beginning with 1. 

All the numbers 1, 3, 5, 7, 9, etc. are called odd numbers. You can keep on counting in this way as long as you want to, and, if you make no mistake, you will be naming odd numbers all the time. You can always tell odd numbers because they end in 1, 3, 5, 7, or 9. 

4. Why do you know that 241 is an odd num-ber? Which of these numbers are odd? Why? 233 234 607 586 865 240 1001 

5. Write five odd numbers. 

6. Write five even numbers. 

7. Name all the odd numbers in this list: 9, 98, 47, 74, 168, 171, 632, 263, 8, 100, 643, 7, 350, 900.

New Ability: To subtract two-digit numbers, no borrowing.

When Ted and his little sister Betty opened their penny banks, they found that Ted had 

87 pennies and Betty had 52 

Betty had 52 pennies. pennies. How many more pennies had Ted than Betty?

This is the way to find out : 

Set the 52 down under the 87, draw a line under them, and subtract. 

2 from 7 is 5. 

5 from 8 is 3. 

Write 5 under the 2. 

Write 3 under the 5.

Ted had 35 more pennies than Betty had. 87 52 35 

Ted asked his mother whether the answer 35 was right. When she solved the problem, she said: "2 and 5 are 7, write 5 under the 2; 5 and 3 are 8, write 3 under the 5. Yes, 35 is right."

GEORGE'S RECORD IN ADDITION FOR A WEEK 

The first column stands for Monday; the second, for Tuesday; the third, for Wednesday; the fourth, for Thursday; and the fifth, for Friday.

On Monday he solved 5 problems correctly in the time given; so he placed a cross before the

THIRD GRADE 

5 in the column for Monday. On Tuesday he solved 6 correctly; so he placed a cross before the 

6 in the column for Tuesday. Then he connected the two records with a line. On Wednesday he had 7 right; so he marked the 7 in the third column and extended the line to it. On Thursday he again had 7 right, so his line was level; but on Friday he had 10 right, so he had to raise the line up to 10 in the fifth column. This line shows at a glance the kind of work that George did with the addition facts that week. You see that he kept the line going up, showing improvement, every day except Thursday, when he stood still. (6 

You may not have a timed test on the addition facts every day as George did, but on many pages in this book there are timed tests arranged in the review lists. You can make a graph sheet like George's on a sheet of paper or, better still, on a notebook page where it will not get lost. 

Head the page 

Addition Facts." Instead of "Monday," write "Test 1"; instead of "Tuesday," write "Test 2," etc. 

Test 2," etc. Each time you are tested, mark your score in the column for that test. Connect your scores with a line and see how soon you can get up to the third-grade standard.

Make a page for the subtraction facts and graph your record for them also.

Later you can start keeping a record for multiplication and one for division.

THIRD GRADE BUYING TOYS FOR THE FISHPOND 

Miss Ward's third grade was going to give a party. Each pupil went to the store and bought two toys to put into a fishpond for the party. 

Each toy cost less than 10¢.

The sign is often used for cents. Thus, 25 cents is written 25¢. 

1. Henry bought a top for 4¢ and a whistle for 34. How much did he pay for both? 

2. Jane bought a box of crayons for 5¢ and a doll for 36. How much did they both cost? 

3. Mary bought some marbles for 6 and a ring for 5¢. How much did she pay in all? 

4. James bought a box of paints for 8¢ and a false face for 5¢. How much did he pay for the two? 

5. Margaret bought a box of dominoes for 9¢ and some jacks for 4¢. How much did she pay in all? 

6. Select two things that you would buy and tell what the total cost would be. 

7. What did you do in each of these problems to find out how much two or more articles cost?

CHECKING ADDITION 

A problem should not be left until the answer has been checked to make sure that it is right. 

To check addition problems, add both ways. Many boys and girls like first to add a problem up, and write the sum over the column. Then they cover the answer and add the problem down, writing the sum under the column. If the two answers are the same, they are probably right. 

New Ability: To add two 5_54065 15 78 42 36 78 15 two-digit numbers when the sum of the tens exceeds 9.

85 Sometimes, when you add the second column, the sum is greater than 9. It has two figures in it. In such cases, be careful to put the right-hand figure, like the 0 in this example, under the column, and the lefthand figure, like the 1 in this example, out at the left by itself. *See Teaching Suggestions, Note 9, page 377. 21 106

CARRYING IN ADDITION

CARRYING IN ADDITION 

New Ability: To recognize and to place correctly units and tens in numbers. 12 14 17 

You can see that 12 is made up of 10 and 2. 

It contains 1 ten and 2 units. 

14 is made up of 10 and 4. It contains 1 ten and 4 units. 

17 is made up of 10 and 7. It contains 1 ten and 7 units. 22 24 27 

You can see also that 22 is made up of 20 and 

2. It contains 2 tens and 2 units. 

24 is made up of 20 and 4. It contains 2 tens and 4 units. 

27 is made up of 20 and 7. It contains 2 tens and 7 units.

THIRD GRADE 

The first digit, or place, on the right always tells how many units there are in a number. 

The second digit, or place, always tells how many tens there are in a number. 

1. How many tens are there in 30? in 40? in 

50? in 60? in 90? 

2. How many units are there in 31? in 44? in 56? in 63? in 92? 

3. Tell the number of tens and units there are in each of the following numbers: 27 71 80 64 85 29 36 75 28 55 70 New Ability: To add two two-digit numbers, carrying from units to tens. 

In some of the addition problems you have solved, the sum of the tens has been larger than 9. Sometimes, also, the sum of the units is larger than 9. The problem below shows you what to do in such a case. 

Adding the units: 8 and 7 are 15. Write the 

5 units under the units and carry the 1 to the tens' column. 27 38 

Adding the tens: 1 carried and 3 and 2 are 6. 

Write the 6 under the tens. 65 

The sum is 6 tens and 5 units, or 65.

When the sum of the units is larger than 9, carry the tens to the tens' column and add them in with the tens.

1. Add the 8 in the center to each number around the rim of the wheel. Starting with 12 and going around like the hands of a clock, the answers are 20, 24, 21, 25, etc. See if you can go all around without making /19 a mistake. Play the game with different numbers in the center.

THIRD GRADE BORROWING IN SUBTRACTION 

In subtraction the larger number is called the minuend. The smaller number is called the subtrahend. The answer is called the difference.

New Ability: To subtract two numbers with borrowing in the units.* 

Find the difference between 63 and 29. 

Subtracting the units: Since you cannot take 9 from 3, take 1 ten from the 6 tens and put it with the units, making 13. 

9 from 13 leaves 4. 

63, Minuend 

29, Subtrahend 

34, Difference 

Subtracting the tens: You have only 5 tens left, since you put one of them with the units. 2 from 5 leaves 3. 

The difference is 34.

Whenever the units in the minuend are less than the units in the subtrahend, borrow a ten * See Teaching Suggestions, Note 13, page 378.

SUBTRACTION New Ability: To check subtraction. 

The difference is what is left of the minuend after taking away the subtrahend. Hence if you add the difference and the subtrahend together, it will give you the minuend. 

If 25 - 8 is 17, then 17+ 8 is 25. Therefore, to check subtraction problems, you add the difference and the subtrahend. If the sum obtained is the same as the minuend, the answer is probably correct.

18 +36 36 54

The difference is 175. 251 175 Whenever the tens in the minuend are less than the tens in the subtrahend, borrow 1 hundred to put with the tens; but don't forget that the hundreds' number will then be 1 smaller.

New Ability: To write cents decimally and to use them in addition and subtraction problems. 

You have learned two ways of writing cents, thus, 50 cents and 50¢. "Fifty cents" may also be written thus: $.50, with a dollar sign and a period called a decimal point. 

The following are written in all three ways: 

1 cent 1¢ 

10 cents 10¢ 

$.01 $.10 

2 cents 2¢ 

15 cents 15¢ $.02 $.15 

20 cents 20¢ $.20 

25 cents 25¢ $.25 

5 cents = 5¢ = $.05 

18 cents 18¢ $.18 

75 cents 75¢ $.75

There are three things to remember in writing cents in the new way: 

1. There must be a dollar sign. 

2. There must be a decimal point. 

3. There must be two digits after the decimal point.

Since there is only one digit in the numbers from 1 cent to 9 cents, you must place a zero after the decimal point to make the two figures, or places, thus: $.04. From 10 cents to 1 dollar there are two digits in all the numbers; hence you do not have to write a zero after the decimal point. 

This way of writing cents is called "writing cents decimally." 

1 dollar may be written in two ways: $1 or $1.00. 5 dollars may be written in two ways: $5 or $5.00. 

1. Write six dollars in two ways; seven dollars; nine dollars. 

2. Write the following decimally: 

3 4 6 9¢ 21¢ 35¢ 8¢ 40¢ 72¢ 80¢ 9¢

When cents are written decimally, the first place to the right of the decimal point really shows the number of dimes. In 34, 6¢, and 

9¢ ($.03, $.06, $.09), the zero shows that there are no dimes. In 20¢ ($.20), there are 2 dimes. 

In 45¢ ($.45) there are 4 dimes, etc.

ST. AR. I- -6

THIRD GRADE 

When cents are written decimally, to be added or subtracted, care must be taken to keep the decimal points in a column, so that the dimes will be in one column and the cents in another column.

When there are dollars and cents to be written decimally, the dollars are written at the left of the decimal point, thus: 5 dollars and 85 cents = $5.85; 5 dollars $5.00. (The zeros show that there are no cents.) Add: $.05 $.25 .24 .37 .36 .50 .10 .06 $.75 $1.18

MAKING CHANGE 

85 

5. Play that you are going to do an errand for your mother and call on some one in the class to tell you what you will have to pay for the three or four things you are going to buy.

MAKING CHANGE * 

Did you ever notice how a clerk counts out change? He doesn't usually give you all the change at once; but he adds the change, coin by coin, to the amount of the purchase. For example, if you buy a pencil for 2¢ and pay with a dime, he will give you first 3 pennies and then a nickel, saying, "2, 5, 10." If you had given him a quarter to pay for the pencil, he would probably have given you for change 3 pennies, a dime, and then another dime, saying, "2, 5, 15, 25." 

New Ability: To count out change by adding.

Count out the change for these bills, which 

Mary paid one day when she went shopping: 

1. She bought a tablet for 10¢ and paid for it with a quarter. 

2. She bought a box of paints for 15¢ and paid for it with two dimes. 

3. She bought an eraser for 5¢ and paid for it with a quarter. 

4. She bought some buttons for her mother for 

13¢ and paid for them with a dime and a nickel. * See Teaching Suggestions, Note 21, page 379.

THIRD GRADE READING AND WRITING NUMBERS 

New Ability: To read and write numbers having three places.*

You have learned that the right-hand figure of a number tells the number of units in the number, and that the second figure tells the number of tens in the number. 

1. How many units and tens are there in 45? in 54? in 82? in 90? in 14?

ROMAN NUMERALS

ROMAN NUMERALS New Ability: To read and write Roman numerals through 20.

Sometimes numbers are written with letters instead of with figures. Some watches have letters instead of figures to mark the hours. 

When numbers are written with letters, they are called Roman numerals. Probably most of you learned the first twelve Roman numerals in the second grade, so that these will be a review; but you must now learn how to read and write all the Roman numerals to 20.

There are just three letters used to write all the numbers to 20. They are I, V, and X. = 1 VI = 6 XI = 11 XVI 16 II 2 

VII 

XII = 12 XVII 17 

III 

= 3 VIII XIII 13 XVIII 18 

IV = 4 

IX XIV 14 XIX 19 V = 5 X = 10 

XV 

15 XX = 20 

You can see that after you have learned the first ten numbers, all you have to do to make the teens is to put an X in front of them. Compare the numbers in the third column with those in the first column. Also compare the numbers in the fourth column with those in the second column.

SHORT DIVISION 

New Ability: To divide three-digit and four-digit numbers by by one digit, all division. 

1. John's father had a flock of 244 chickens. even One day he told John that he was going to sell half of them. How many did he plan to sell?

This is the way John found out how many chickens his father was going to sell: 

He divided the hundreds' number by 2 (2÷ 2 = 1) and set the 1 in the hundreds' column. 

He divided the tens' number by 2 (4÷÷ 2 = 2) and set the 2 in the tens' column. 2)244 122 He divided the units' number by 2 (4÷ 2 = 2) and set the 

2 in the units' column. 

John found that his father was going to sell 122 chickens.

SUBTRACTION New Ability: To subtract four-digit numbers. 

Subtract 3927 from 8152. 

Subtracting the units: 12 less 7 equals 5. 

Subtracting the tens: 4 less 2 equals 2. 

Subtracting the hundreds: 11 less 9 equals 2. 

Subtracting the thousands: 7 less 3 equals 4. 

The difference is 4225.

SHORT DIVISION 

New Ability: To solve short-division problems with three or four digits, including one or two zeros, in the quotient. 

Sometimes it is necessary to use two numbers instead of one in the middle of an example in order to divide, as in 9)927. In this case you 103 must put a zero under the number 2, which you cannot divide, before using it with the next number, 7. Notice the zeros in the following quotients: 

Examples. 9)918 102 9)2727 303 9)9036 1004 

In the first example, 9 is not contained in 1; so 0 is written under the 1 and the 1 used with the next number, 8, making 18; 9 is contained 2 times in 18.

THIRD GRADE 

In the second example, 9 is contained in 27, 3 times; but it is not contained in 2, so 0 is written under the 2 and 9 is divided into 27. 

In the third example, the first 0 is the quotient of 0 divided by 9, and the second 0 is the quotient of 3 divided by 9. The 4 is the quotient of 36 divided by 9. Divide:

READING AND WRITING NUMBERS * New Ability: To read and write numbers tended to thousands. 

1. Tell the number of units, tens, and hundreds in each of these numbers: 468, 321, 602, 700, 505, 110, 328, 636, 333.

The fourth figure, or place, in a number shows the number of thousands in the number. In 4760 there are 4 thousands. The number 4760 is read "4 thousand 7 hundred 60." In 1065 there is 1 thousand. The number 1065 is read "1 thousand 65." *See Teaching Suggestions, Note 23, page 379.

New Ability: To carry from tens tens to hundreds when adding 3-digit numbers. 

Add 246, 324, 352, and 464. 

You learned that when you have two digits in the sum of the units, you carry the left-hand figure to the tens' column. When the sum of the tens has two digits in it, you carry the left-hand figure to the hundreds' column. 246 324 352 464 1386 In this example, the sum of the units is 16; so you write the 6 under the units and carry the 1 to the tens. 

Adding the 1 to the other tens makes the sum of the tens 18. 

Write the 8 under the tens and carry the 1 to the hundreds.

Adding the 1 to the other hundreds makes the sum of the

ADDITION 

123 hundreds 13. Write the 3 under the hundreds and the 1 out at the left. The sum is 1386. 

Add up, and check by adding down. Don't forget to add the number you carry to the next column: 1. 456 230 2. 345 3. 234

THE TWINS GO SHOPPING 

The Glenwood Dry-goods Company was having a sale. Jack and Jill went with their mother to buy some things at the sale prices. 

1. Jill bought some dress goods for $1.95, some lace to trim the dress for $.56, a pair of shoes for $2.45, and some socks for $.29. What was the total bill for her clothes?

THIRD GRADE 

2. Jack bought a cap for $.65, a blouse for $1.15, some stockings for $.35, and a coat for $6.95. What was Jack's bill? 

3. Their mother bought a pair of gloves for $1.95, some material for a dress for $5.40, a hat for $5.25, and an umbrella for $3.50. What was her bill? 

4. When they were through shopping, Jill had a dish of ice cream, which cost 15¢; Jack had an ice-cream soda, which cost 10; and their mother had ice cream with nuts, which cost 20¢. 

How much was their bill?

THE CALENDAR 

New Ability: To name all the months of the year in order, to write their correct abbreviations, and to give the number of days in each month. 

Every pupil in the third grade should be able to name all the months of the year in their right order. Can you do this? If you are not sure about some of them, study them until you can't miss them. 

January (Jan.) 

31 da. 

February (Feb.) 28 da. July 

31 da. 

August (Aug.) 

31 da. or 29 da. 

September (Sept.) 30 da. 

March (Mar.) 

31 da. 

October (Oct.) 

31 da. 

April (Apr.) 

30 da. 

November (Nov.) 30 da. 

31 da. 

30 da. May June 

Sometimes, when you are writing the name of a month, you haven't room for the whole name. 

You may then write part of the name, called an 

December (Dec.) 31 da.

THIRD GRADE 

abbreviation. The correct abbreviation, showing the capital and the period, which you must never forget, is written in a parenthesis after the name of the month. You will notice that for each abbreviation the first three or four letters of the name are used.

The number of days in each month is written after the name of the month. If you do not already know how many days there are in each month, this rime will help you: 

Thirty days hath September, April, June, and November. 

All the rest have thirty-one 

Except February alone, Which has but twenty-eight in fine 

Till leap year brings it twenty-nine. 

1. Name all the months of the year. 

2. Name the school months. 

3. Name the vacation months. 

4. Write the date of your birthday. 

5. Write the following dates, abbreviating the names of the months: New Year's Day; St. Valentine's Day; Independence Day; Christmas; St. 

Patrick's Day.

THIRD GRADE TELLING TIME 

New Ability: To know the meaning of terms in the time represented by the spaces on a clock face. 12 11 10 2 3 

1. What kind of figures does the face of your clock at home have?

The kind of figures we generally use in arithmetic problems are called Arabic numerals. 

2. What are numbers made from letters called?

Some clocks have Roman numerals and some have Arabic numerals. 

3. Write the Arabic numbers from 1 to 12.

Beside each one write the Roman numeral that means the same. 

4. How many numbers are there on a clock face?

The short hand on the clock is called the hour hand. The long hand is called the minute hand. 

5. How long does it take the hour hand to go around the clock face? How long does it take the minute hand?

TELLING TIME 

137 Generally, when we use the word day, we mean the light part of the day, and we call the dark part of the day night. The real length of a day, however, is 24 hours and it is part light and part dark. The new day starts at midnight and lasts until the next midnight. 

6. What time is midnight? What hour is noon? Why are there two four o'clocks and two eight o'clocks in one day? 

7. How many times does the hour hand go around the clock face in one day? How many times does the minute hand go around the clock face in one hour? 

8. How long does it take the minute hand to pass between two numbers? the hour hand? 

9. When the minute hand points to 12 and the hour hand points to 8, what time is it? 

10. Where is the minute hand at 10 minutes after 8? Where is it at 25 minutes after 8? 

Where is it at 25 minutes to 9? 

11. When the minute hand points to 8 and the hour hand points to 12, what time is it? 

12. Where is the minute hand at half past 4? 

Where is it at quarter to 6? 

13. What time do the clock faces on page 136 tell? 

14. Draw a clock face on the board and have the class tell what time you have represented.

MULTIPLICATION 

New Ability: To multiply a two-digit or a threedigit number, with carrying from the units to the tens. Add: 

When 6 and 6 are added to make 12, the 2 is written under the units and the 1 is carried to the tens' column. Multiply: 26 26 26 2 52 52 When 6 is multiplied by 2 and the product is 12, the 2 is written under the units and the 1 is carried over and added to the tens. Multiplying 2 tens by 

2 gives 4. Add the 1 carried from the units, and write 5. 

2 times 26 gives 52, just as 26 plus 26 is 52.

In multiplication, it is very important to keep the columns straight. Multiply: 1. 13 2. 25 3. 24 354 

4. 47 

5. 26 6. 36 2 2 

7. 45 8. 67 

9. 83 10. 74 11. 92 12. 88 2 2 Multiply: 

Often there is more than 1 to carry from the units to the tens. In this problem, 4 times 8 are 

32. Write the 2 under the units and carry the 3 to the tens. 4 times 3 are 12 and 12 plus the 3 carried from the units makes 15. Write 15. 

The product is 152.

THIRD GRADE 

The answers to multiplication problems should be checked, so that perfect papers may be handed in.

Multiplication problems should be checked by solving them a second time. The best plan is not to look at the first solution, but to solve the problem as though it were a new problem. 

Then compare the two answers to see if they agree.

New Ability: To multiply a three-digit number and carry from the tens to the hundreds. 

Often, when the tens are multiplied, there are two digits in the product. In that case the righthand digit is set down under the tens and the lefthand digit is carried to the hundreds and added to its product.

The product is 3368.

MEASURING TEMPERATURE * 

New Ability To be able to tell the temperature by reading a thermometer. 

If you want to know how warm or how cold a room is, you look at the thermometer. The thermometer shows the number of degrees of heat or cold. 

The liquid in the tube of the thermometer rises and falls with the temperature of the room. Each line of the thermometer shows two degrees of heat. thermometer at the right reads 76 degrees. 

Your hand is warmer than the air in the room. Hold it tightly over the bulb of the thermometer and see whether heat makes the mercury go up or down. 90 70 70 100 80 60 50 The 40 30 20 10 10 10 

السلسيلي 

Notice that there is a point on the thermometer that is marked 0, and that the degrees are numbered above and below 0. 32 degrees above 0 is the freezing point; so you know that temperatures below 0 are very cold. *See Teaching Suggestions, Note 31, page 380.

THIRD GRADE 

1. The correct temperature for a schoolroom is about 68 degrees. See if the temperature in your schoolroom is right. 

2. Below what point would you expect the temperature to be in an ice-manufacturing plant? 

3. At what temperature might there be danger of freezing the water pipes? 

4. If the temperature was 58 degrees this morning and 72 degrees at noon, was it warmer or colder at noon? How many degrees warmer or colder was it?

The sign means degree or degrees. 

5. Can you skate on the lake when the temperature outdoors is 35°? when it is 25°? 

6. Put your schoolroom thermometer outdoors and see if it is warmer or colder outdoors than indoors. First guess how many degrees of difference there are; then read the thermometer and see who guessed nearest.

SHORT DIVISION

SHORT DIVISION 

New Ability: To check short-division problems.

In division problems, the number that is divided is called the dividend. The number by which the dividend is divided is called the divisor. The answer is called the quotient. 

All division problems should be checked to see if the quotient is correct. To check a division problem, multiply the quotient by the divisor. If the product thus obtained is the same number as the dividend, the work is probably correct.

5. Jack went on a camping trip with some boys.

He had $15 when he started and $4.65 when he came back. How much did he spend on the trip? 

6. Mary's mother paid her 10¢ for each window that she washed. There were 24 windows in their house and Mary washed all of them. 

How much did she earn? 

7. Mary saved the money earned in problem 6 to buy a pair of roller skates that cost $2.75. 

How much more money did she need? 

8. Dick and Don sold 5 boxes of blackberries for 25¢ a box. How much did they get for them? 

9. Seven boys went on a camping trip. The whole expense was $29.40. If the boys shared the expense equally, how much was each boy's share?

THIRD GRADE MAKING A GARDEN 

John and Ted are brothers. One year their father told them that if they wanted to earn some money, they could make a garden. Here are some of the problems they had to solve. 

1. How much do 9 packages of seeds cost at 5¢ a package? 

2. How much do 7 packages cost at 10¢ a package? 

3. How much do 6 packages cost at 15¢ a package? 

4. How much do 3 hundred cabbage plants cost at 25¢ a hundred? 

5. If there are 45 potato plants in a row, how many plants are there in 9 rows? 

6. When John and Ted had hoed 200 potato plants, how many more plants did they have to hoe? (See problem 5.)

9. Some of their watermelons they sold for 

35¢ apiece. How much did they get for 5 watermelons of this size?

SHORT DIVISION 

New Ability: To solve and check short-division problems having a remainder in the quotient. 

75 divided by 8 is 9, with a remainder of 3. 

Write 9. 

Carry the 3 to the units. 36 divided by 

8 is 4, with a remainder of 4. Write 4. 

Then write the remainder 4 after the quotient with an r for remainder. 

To check the division to see if 94, 4 r is the correct answer, multiply 94 by 8 and add 4.

Since the product is the same as the dividend, 756, the check shows that the work is correct.

Example 

8)756 

94, 4 r Check 94 752 756

PROBLEMS WITHOUT NUMBERS 

Answer each question by telling whether you would add, subtract, multiply, or divide, and give a problem to illustrate each. 

1. If you know the price of an article, how can you find the price of several articles just like it? 

2. If you know how much a bag of sugar weighs, how can you find how much several bags of sugar of the same size weigh?

ST. AR. I-12

THIRD GRADE 

3. If you know the price of an article, how can you find how many such articles you could buy for a certain sum of money? 

4. If you know how much money you start with and how much you have left after shopping, how can you find out how much money you have spent? 

5. If you know how much money you have and how much an article costs, how can you find out how much money you will have left after buying the article? 

6. If you know how much money you have spent for each of several articles, how can you find how much you have spent for all together? 

7. If you know how much each of two articles costs, how can you find how much more one costs than the other? 

8. If you have a flock of chickens and want to sell one third of them, how can you find how many chickens that will be? 

9. If you want to buy a book at a certain price, but haven't enough money, how can you find how much more money you need? 

10. If you know how many yards of cloth it takes to make a dress, how can you find how many yards it takes for several similar dresses?

THIRD GRADE WORDS YOU USE IN ARITHMETIC 

abbreviation: a shortened form of writing words digit any of the figures smaller than 10 dividend: a number to be divided by another number divisor: the number by which you divide another even number: any number which can be divided by 2 without leaving a remainder fraction: one or more parts of a unit graph: a diagram which shows the difference between things in a kind of picture (see page 29) multiplicand: a number which is to be multiplied by another number multiplier: a number by which you multiply another number minuend: the number from which you subtract another number odd number: any number which cannot be divided by 2 without leaving a remainder product: the answer to a multiplication problem quotient: the answer to a division problem remainder: the answer to a subtraction problem, and also the number which is left over when a division problem does not come out even Roman numerals: a way of writing numbers with letters instead of figures subtrahend: the number which is to be subtracted from another number sum: the answer to an addition problem unit: the number 1

FOURTH GRADE OBJECTIVES FOR THE FOURTH GRADE 1. Reading and Writing Numbers 

To read and write Arabic numbers extended to 1,000,000,000.

To read and write Roman numerals extended to 199. 

2. Fundamental Processes 

To add single columns extended to 9 addends. 

To add multiple columns extended to 

6 addends 3 digits wide; and to 4 addends 4 digits wide. 

To check solutions to addition problems.

To subtract problems extended to fivedigit numbers, involving all types of borrowing. 

To check solutions to subtraction problems.

To solve short-multiplication problems with four digits in the multiplicand.

To solve long-multiplication problems extended to four-digit multiplicands and three-digit multipliers. 

To check solutions to multiplication problems.

To solve short-division problems extended to five-digit dividends. 185

FOURTH GRADE 

To solve long-division problems to four-digit dividends and to two-digit divisors. 

To check solutions to division problems. 

3. United States Money 

To read and write numbers to $1,000,000.00. 

To apply the four fundamental processes, numbers in the problems to be more than five digits wide. 

4. Denominate Numbers

To reduce and use in practical problems the units of measurement in the following measures: United States 

States money, time, dry, liquid, avoirdupois weight, length, and square measure. 

5. Fractions

To understand the relation of fractions and integers.

To understand the relation of halves and fourths; of halves, fourths, and eighths; of halves and sixths; of thirds and sixths. 

To find fractional parts of integers, as, of 12, of 12.

To add and subtract halves and halves, sixths and sixths, eighths and eighths, etc. 

6. Concrete Problems 

To attack independently and solve onestep and two-step problems, within the experiences of fourth-grade pupils.

FOURTH GRADE HOW TO SOLVE PROBLEMS * 

Much of the trouble in solving concrete problems will be avoided if from the beginning a plan for attacking them is used and if checking habits are built up. Estimating the answer in advance is one good check. Another is looking at an answer critically and asking, "Is my answer reasonable or is it too large or too small?" 

The following steps are suggested: 

1. Always read the problem through carefully. 

2. See what you are asked to find. 

3. See what is given in the problem. 

4. Plan the solution. 

5. Solve the problem. 

6. Check the solution and the answer. 

1. What does the word total mean? In each of the following problems you are to find a total. 

2. In the doll show at school the girls had 12 dolls with yellow hair, 16 dolls with brown hair, and 19 dolls with black hair. How many dolls were there in the show? 

3. Bob was trying to throw stones into a tin can. 

He got 19 stones into the can, but he missed 8 times. How many stones did Bob throw? 

4. Jane's mother used 16 yd. of material to make curtains. She had 5 yd. left over. many yards did she have all together? * See Teaching Suggestions, Note 2, page 381. How

HOW TO SOLVE PROBLEMS 

205 

5. Jane went shopping with her mother one

Saturday. They bought a pair of shoes for $3.50, some ribbon for $.75, material for a dress for $4.65, and a spool of thread for $.06. What was the total bill? 

6. The children of the fourth grade are going to have a picnic. There are 19 children in one fourth-grade room, and there are 23 children in the other fourth-grade room. How many children will there be at the picnic? 

7. Ed's herd of cows gave 21 gallons of milk on Monday, 19 gallons on Tuesday, 20 gallons on Wednesday, and 18 gallons on Thursday. How many gallons did they give in those four days? 

8. The fourth grade wanted to give a play. 

They found that they would have to pay for four things. Material for the costumes would cost $3.25, the wires for the lights would cost $.65, the printing of the programs would cost $1.25, and the wood for the scenery would cost $2. How much would it cost to give the play? 

9. They gave the play three times. The first time 82 people came to see it. The next time 

60 people came to see it, and the third time 91 people came. What is the total number of people who saw the fourth-grade play? 

10. What do you do to find a total?

FOURTH GRADE 

4. Write: 3 thousand 6; 4 thousand 3 hundred 5; 

5 thousand 82; 9 thousand 2 hundred 99; 4 thousand 

3 hundred seventy-six. 

The fourth, fifth, and sixth digits in a number name thousands. The fourth place or number is single thousands (4000 is read "4 thousand"; 7000 is read "7 thousand"); the fifth place names tenthousands (12,000 is read "12 thousand "; 22,000 is read "22 thousand," etc.); the sixth place names hundred-thousands (136,000 is read "136 thousand"; that is, it is 1 hundred-thousand and 

36 thousands). With a number of more than four figures, a comma is placed before the hundreds figure as a help in reading the number. 43,446;

New Ability: To recognize an inch and a foot, and to use them in making measurements. 

12 inches (in.) 

1 foot (ft.) 

3 feet 1 yard (yd.) 36 inches 1 yard 

16 feet 

1 rod (rd.) 

5 yards 1 rod 

5280 feet 

1 mile (mi.)

320 rods = 1 mile 

1. Draw on paper or on the board a line which you think is 1 in. long. Measure it with your ruler to see how nearly right you were. 

2. Guess how long the cover of your arithmetic is. Then measure it to see how nearly right your guess was. 

3. Guess how many inches wide your arithmetic is. Measure it to see whether you guessed right.

Measure it to see whether you guessed right. the length of 

5. Guess and then measure the a box. 

6. Guess and then measure the length of the pencil you are using. 

7. Draw on the board a line that you think is 1 ft. long. Measure it with your ruler to see if you were right. 

8. Draw on the board a line about 18 in. long. 

Measure it with your ruler to see if you were right. 

9. Guess the width of the blackboard in feet and inches; then measure it. 

10. Guess the length and the width of your desk top; then measure it. 

11. Guess how many feet and inches from the floor your desk top is; then measure it. 

12. Draw on the board, or measure off on the floor, a line that is 1 rd. long. Measure the line with your ruler to see how many feet and yards there are in it. 

13. Find how many rods and feet long your school building is. Find its width in rods and feet. 

14. Measure other things in rods and feet.

"Go around the wheel" with the other pupils and see who can make the best score. 

You may 7 39 2 (4x) 1 have three trials. If all your answers are right the first time, you score 3 points; if a second trial is needed, you score 2 points; if a third trial is needed, you score 1 point. 

The pupil with the highest score wins.

New Ability: To recognize a yard and to use it in making measurements. 

A unit of measure that you will use very often is the yard. 

1. How many things can you name that are bought by the yard?

1 yd. long. Measure the line with a yardstick to see how nearly right you were. 

3. How many yards long do you think the schoolroom is? Measure it to see whether you are right. 

4. How far from the floor do you think the bottom of the blackboard is? Measure it to see whether you are right.

FOURTH GRADE PROBLEMS IN MEASURING 

1. If Frank's father steps twice as far each step as Frank does, how many steps will Frank have to take to go as far as his father does in 20 steps? 

2. Glenn's bicycle goes 7 ft. at each turn. 

How many feet will he go if the wheel turns 

16 times? How many yards is that? 

3. If a lot is 75 ft. wide, how many yards wide is it? 

4. A room is 18 ft. long and 16 ft. wide. How many feet of molding will be necessary to inclose it? 

5. How many yards are there in 2 mi.? 

6. A field is 96 rd. long and 56 rd. wide.

How many rods of fencing will be necessary to inclose it? 

7. The walk from the house to the gate is 

624 in. long. If bricks are 8 in. long, how many bricks, laid end to end, will it take to reach from the house to the gate? 

8. In problem 7, if the bricks are laid side by side and are 4 in. wide, how many bricks will it take to reach from the house to the gate? 

9. If in problem 7 there are 4 rows of bricks, how many bricks will it take to make the walk? 

10. If there are 5280 ft. in 1 mi., how many feet are there in mi.? How many yards are there in mi.?

AREAS OF RECTANGLES 

New Ability: To recognize square inches and square feet.

The corners of a rectangle are called angles. 

The four lines bounding the rectangle are called sides. The space which the sides bound is called the surface, or the area. 

If we wish to buy linoleum, or paint a floor, or make a concrete sidewalk, or sod a lawn, we need to find areas of rectangles. 

Surfaces or areas are measured by square units square inches, square feet, square yards, etc. * See Teaching Suggestions, Note 10, page 382. 

---

FOURTH GRADE 

The figure at the right is a square inch. It is a square measuring an inch on each side.

Figures of a square foot and a square yard would look just like this, except that they would be larger. A square foot measures a foot on each side, and a square yard measures a yard on each side. 

A SQUARE INCH 

144 square inches (sq. in.) = 1 square foot (sq. ft.) 

9 square feet = 1 square yard (sq. yd.) 

640 acres (A.) = 1 square mile (sq. mi.)

The square unit that is used to measure a piece of land, such as a farm, is the acre. A very large piece of land, such as a state, would be measured in square miles. A section of land contains 1 square mile, or 640 acres. 

1. Take a ruler and draw a square foot on the blackboard. 

2. If your blackboard is wide enough, take a yardstick and draw a square yard on the board. 

3. When you have drawn a square yard, mark it off into square feet. 

4. Mark each square foot off into square inches. 

5. How many square inches 

2 sq. ft.?

AREAS OF RECTANGLES 

New Ability: To find the area of a rectangle when the length and the width are given. 

The figure at the left, ABCD, is a rectangle 6 ft. long and 4 ft. wide. To find the number of square 4 ft. 

1 sq. ft. feet in the area, you can think that there one are 6 sq. ft. in strip along the end (strip AD) and that

6 ft. there are 4 strips. 

Then in the whole rectangle you see that there are 4 × 6 sq. ft., or 24 sq. ft. Or you can look at the strip of square feet along a side (strip AB) and think that there are 4 sq. ft. in one strip, and that there are 6 strips like it in the whole rectangle; so there must be 6 x 4 sq. ft., or 

Either way, the area of the rectangle is 

24 sq. ft. 

24 sq. ft.

In a rectangle 8 in. long and 6 in. wide there are 6 x 8 sq. in. or 8 x 6 sq. in., which are 

48 sq. in.

12. 8412 GRAPHS 

New Ability: To read and make bar graphs.

How JOHN SHOWS HIS PROGRESS IN SPELLING 

In tests of 20 words, John spelled 8 words correctly on Monday, 12 words on Tuesday, 10 on 

Wednesday, 16 on Thursday, and 18 on Friday.

On page 247 you will find a picture of his record for the week.

Where are the figures showing the number of words? What shows the number of words spelled correctly on Monday? on Tuesday? on each of the other days?

This picture is called a bar graph. 

You can get paper printed with little squares as in this graph; but it is not difficult to make squared paper from a ruled tablet. Your paper already has the lines drawn across it; so all you

GRAPHS 

247 have to do is to draw the vertical lines, starting about an inch from the left-hand margin. Make your lines as far apart as the lines that are already on the paper, so that you will have squares. Continue the lines to the right-hand margin.

Many fourth-grade children keep a picture of their record in some of their subjects, in order that they may see at a glance how much progress they are making. In spelling, if they have the same number of words to spell each day, they make a graph showing the number of words spelled correctly each day. If just one list is given each week, then the record may be kept for several weeks. 

Number of Words Correct

The pupils in Miss Clark's grade were anxious to come up to their grade standard in short division so that they could go on to long division. The standard was 4 problems in 3 minutes. Every day for a week Miss Clark timed the pupils and they made graphs to show their records. The numbers of problems which some of the pupils could solve in 3 minutes are given below. 6. Fanny 2 Tues. Wed. Thurs. Fri. 2 42 2 332 + 32 213 34 7. Sometimes other things than school records are shown by graphs. A graph can be made to show how these three sisters compare in height:

Mary is 5 ft. tall, Jane is 4 ft. tall, and Edna is 

3 ft. tall. Number the horizontal lines at the left from 0 to 6. Each space will then stand for 

1 foot. Label three vertical bars with the girls' names, starting with the tallest. (See the names of the days in the graph on page 247.) Then draw each bar up to the horizontal line containing the number that represents each girl's height. This graph will show how the three girls compare in height.

LONG MULTIPLICATION 

New Ability: To multiply by a two-digit number.

The fourth grade wanted to make some toy money. They decided that each pupil needed 32 coins. There were 28 pupils in the class. How many coins did they need to make? 

When there are two digits in the multiplier, the multiplication is made first by the units' number in the multiplier and then by the tens' number, just as for two short multiplication problems. The only new thing to be careful about is that the right-hand digit in each of these partial products is written under the number by which you are multiplying. 32 28 256 64 896 In the example at the right, 8 times 32 are 256. The 6 is written under the 8 of 28. 2 times 32 are 64. The 4 is written under the 2 of 28.

Adding the two partial products gives 896 for the total product.

The class needed to make 896 coins.

MAKING PROBLEMS † 

From every subtraction problem, an addition problem can be made; and from every addition problem, one or more subtraction problems can be made. When one problem is thus made from another, we call it a related problem. 

A. Subtraction Problem. John had 20 marbles and lost 5 marbles. How many marbles had he left? (Ans. 15 marbles)

New Ability: To multiply by a two-digit number with a zero in the units' place. 

One day the fourth grade had an arithmetic race. They had 10 multiplication problems to solve, in each of which the multiplier ended in *See Teaching Suggestions, Note 26 - Drill Device, page 384.

a zero. 

Milton, a new boy, finished long before any one else. When he showed the class that he really had solved every problem, they saw that he had saved time by writing his problems as in (b) below, while the rest of the class had written theirs as in (a).

When any number is multiplied by zero, the product is zero. 

In example (a), 0 times 248 is 000. 6 times 

248 are 1488. Add the partial products. 

The total product is 14,880. (a) 248 

To save time, problems like this may be written as in example (b). Write a zero in the (b) product under the zero in the multiplier and then proceed to multiply by the next digit.

LONG DIVISION 

271 Divide these problems, writing out the work in full: * 

9. 8)3656 10. 7)4585 11. 6)5868 12. 9)5675 

13. 3)2463 14. 6)5724 

15. 9)7182

21. 6)4895 22. 8)3848 23. 7)6587 

24. 9)4869 

25. 8)6592 26. 4)3588 27. 5)2585 28. 8)4683 

These are the four steps in long division, repeated until the last digit in the dividend has been used: 

1. Estimate the number for the quotient. 

2. Multiply the divisor by this number. 

3. Subtract this product from the partial dividend. 

4. Bring down the next digit in the dividend to the right of the remainder.

Lucile's father has a stationery store. Lucile earns her spending money by helping in the store on Saturdays and some evenings. These are some of the problems she had to solve one Saturday: 

1. A man came in and bought a bottle of paste for 45¢, a box of paper clips for 10¢, and a notebook for 15¢. What was the amount of the purchase? 

2. He gave Lucile a dollar bill to pay for his purchases. What change should she give him? 

3. A neighbor came in to buy a Saturday 

Evening Post. Lucile said, "Mr. Smith, you pay 5¢ each week for 52 weeks, which makes your Saturday Evening Post cost $2.60 a year. 

If you would give me your subscription for a year, it would cost you only $2.00; so you would save 60¢." Was she right?

New Ability: To multiply by a three-digit multiplier. 

When there are three digits in the multiplier, the problem is like three short multiplication problems.

Each partial product is set down under the number by which you multiply. In this example, 6 times 1468 is 8808. The 8 at the right of 8808 is set down under the 6 of 236. 1468 236 8808 4404 2936 346448

Adding the 3 partial products gives the total product, 346,448. 

The 6 of 2936 is set down under the 2 of

Be careful that each partial product is set down one place to the left of the one before it.

ADDING AND SUBTRACTING DOLLARS AND CENTS 

When cents are written with a decimal point and a dollar sign, there must be two figures to show the number of cents. If only one figure is needed to express the number, a zero is used in front of the figure to fill out the two places. 4¢ is written $.04; 12¢ is written $.12.

LONG DIVISION 

New Ability: To estimate the quotient quotient figure when the divisor ends with zero and the division is even. 

How many 

At a class picnic, Nancy said, "We have 

60 sandwiches and there are 20 of us. sandwiches can we put on each plate?"

LONG DIVISION 

287 She didn't know how to divide by 20 because this divisor has two figures.

Nancy's teacher told her to work this problem by long division.

This is the way the teacher explained it: 

When a two-figure divisor ends in zero, look at the tens' digit only and decide the number of times that it will divide into the first number of the dividend. 3 20)60 60

In this problem, 2 will divide into 6, 3 times; so you know that 3 is the figure to write in the quotient. The 3 is written over the 0. 3 times 20 is 60. If you subtract 60 from 60 there is no remainder, so the answer is 3. 

They could put 3 sandwiches on each plate. Divide: 

1. 30)90 2.40)80 3. 20)40 4. 10)70 5. 30)60

Quite often the tens' figure will not divide into the first figure of the dividend. Then the first two figures must be used. 

In this problem, 5 will divide into 45, 9 times; 9 50)450 450 so 9 is written over the 0. 9 times 50 is 450. Subtracting 450 from 450 leaves no remainder. Therefore the answer is 9; that is, 450 divided by 50 is 9. Divide:

BUYING AND SELLING 

New Ability: To understand and use the terms profit and loss.

If a person is in the business of buying and selling and sells something for more than he pays for it, we say that he makes money on the sale or that he makes a profit, or or a gain. If he sells an article for less money than he pays

BUYING AND SELLING 

291 for it, he loses money on the trade. The amount of money that he loses is called his loss. 

1. Sue's father bought a cow for $60, and he sold it for $50. How much money did he lose on the sale of the cow? 

2. The man who owns the toy store buys roller skates for $2.10 a pair, and sells them for $2.50 a pair. 

How much does he gain on each pair of skates? 

3. At the end of the summer the man in the toy store wants to sell all the roller skates he has left.

He sells them at $2 a pair. How much does he lose on each pair of skates he sells at this price (problem 2)? 

4. Jim paid 95¢ for a book which he wanted to read. The book dealer had paid 69¢ for the book. 

How much did the dealer make on Jim's book? 

5. Jane's father is in the real estate business.

He bought a house for $4500 and sold it for $4200. 

Did he make a profit or a loss? How much profit or loss did he make? 

6. The next week Jane's father bought a house for $3250 and sold it for $3600. Did he make a profit or a loss on this sale? How much profit or loss did he make? 

7. Mr. Black, who is a grocer, buys lard at 78¢ a can and sells it at 85¢ a can. How much does he gain on each can?

MEASURING DISTANCE IN AN AUTOMOBILE

MEASURING TIME 

New Ability: To understand and to use measures of time. 

60 seconds (sec.) 

1 minute (min.) 

60 minutes 1 hour (hr.) 

24 hours 1 day (da.) 

7 days 1 week (wk.) 

365 days 

1 year (yr.) 

12 months (mo.) 

1 year

June? in October? in February? in May?

7. How many minutes are there in hr.? in hr.? in 2 hr.?

FOURTH GRADE 

8. How many seconds are there in min.? in min.? 

9. What hour of the day is called noon?

Sometimes, to show that the time is noon instead of midnight, the letter M is used, thus: 

12 M.

The hours after midnight and before noon are marked A.M. Thus, 10 A.M. is ten o'clock in the morning (before noon). The hours after noon up to midnight are marked P.M. Thus, 10 P.M. is ten o'clock at night.

When we write both hours and minutes, we use a period to separate the minutes from the hours. Thus, 30 minutes past ten in the morning (half past ten) may be written 10.30 a.m., and 15 minutes past three in the afternoon (quarter past three) may be written 3.15 P.M.

1.00 A.M., 3.15 P.M., 4.55 A.M., 6.30 A.M., 12.00 M., 8.10 A.M., 7.45 P.M., 12.30 A.M., 12.30 P.M.

14 1/2 1/4 MIN 1/2 1/2 12 114 14 A unit is the whole of any thing. Sometimes a unit is a single thing, like an apple or a stick of candy or a dollar. Sometimes it is a group of things. For example, all your money is a unit, all the oranges in a sack are a unit, all the pupils in a class are a unit, etc. 

A part of a unit is called a fraction. * See Teaching Suggestions, Note 36, page 385.

New Ability: To solve long-division problems when the estimated quotient figure is 10. 

Although 4 will divide into 4 once, you see at a glance that 43 will not divide into 41. Taking the first two figures of the dividend, you find that 41 ÷ 4 10; but as 9 is the largest figure that can be written in the quotient, you write 9 over 7. 9 X 43 387; 417-387 97 43)4171 387 301 301 = 30. As 30 is smaller than 43, 9 is the correct quotient figure. 

Bring down 1, giving 301 for the new partial dividend. 

30 ÷ 4 = 7. Write 7 over the 1. 7 X 43 = 301. There is no remainder.

Hence the quotient is 97.

DRAWING TO SCALE 

New Ability: To read scales on maps and to make simple drawings to a scale. 

Did you ever have your picture taken? Was it as big as you are? Was it half as big? Did it look like you?

Often drawings are made of such things as rooms, buildings, parks, farms, etc., which are exactly the same shape and proportions as the original, but much smaller. 

1. Take a sheet of paper 8 in. by 11 in. and on it make a drawing to represent the sheet of paper. Make it as large as the real sheet. 

How long will your drawing be? how wide?

FOURTH GRADE 

2. Make another drawing of the same sheet of paper which is as large as the sheet. How long will the drawing be? how wide? is 

3. The stand top on which the goldfish bowl in the picture is set 12 in. square. In a drawing which is 3 in. square, how many inches of top will 1 in. of your drawing represent? In a drawing which is 2 in. square, how many inches of top will 1 in. of your drawing represent?

We may make a map, or a drawing, of an object smaller than the real object, but it must have the same proportions; that is, every inch in the drawing must represent a certain length of the object drawn. This is called drawing to scale. 

4. One fourth-grade room in the Washington

School is 24 ft. wide and 30 ft. long. Make a drawing to represent the floor of the room, using the scale 1 in. 6 ft. (That is, 1 in. of your drawing should represent 6 ft. of the floor.) 5. Grandfather's potato patch is 25 yd. long and 20 yd. wide. Make a drawing of the patch to the scale 1 in. = 5 yd.

LONG DIVISION 

327 6. An athletic field is 100 yd. square. If a drawing of it is 5 in. square, what is the scale? 

7. A barn lot is 120 ft. by 160 ft. Draw it to a scale of 1 in. to 20 ft. 

8. A township is 6 mi. square. Make a drawing of convenient size to represent it and tell what scale you used. 

9. Suppose your county is 35 mi. long and 

20 mi. wide. Make a drawing of it and tell what scale you used. 

10. A garden is 90 ft. by 150 ft. Make a drawing of it and tell what scale you used. 

11. See what scale is used for a map of your state in your geography. 

12. On the map, measure the greatest length and the greatest width of your state. Using the scale given on the map, estimate the length and the width of the state in miles. 

LONG DIVISION New Ability: To solve long-division problems with two-digit divisors which are in the middle of the decade. 

These are hard divisors for which to estimate quotient figures. It frequently takes several trials before the right figure is found. By doing some mental estimating, time can be saved.

FOURTH GRADE 

In this problem, 2 will divide 8 times into 17; but 

As 208 is larger than 176, try 7 for the quotient 

8 X 26 = 208. figure. 7 × 26

6 X 26 = 156. 

182. As 182 is also larger than 176, try 6. 

156 is smaller than 176 and leaves 20 for a remainder. Therefore 6 is the right quotient figure. 68 26)1768 156 

As 234 is 208 208 

Try the largest figure, 9, for the next quotient figure. 9 X 26 234. larger than 208, try 8. 8 X 26 208. There is no remainder. 

Hence the quotient is 68.

The only hard thing about these problems is that we must sometimes try several quotient figures before the right one is found. To do these problems quickly, it is necessary to learn how to estimate the right figure quickly. See in how many of the following problems you can estimate the right quotient figures.

FINDING THE AVERAGE 

New Ability: To find the average when the total is given.

PREPARING FOR A CHRISTMAS PARTY 

1. A class of 25 pupils collected $6.25 for 

Christmas party. How much did each child give if each gave the same amount? 

Another way of asking this question is, "What was the average amount that each child gave? 

The average amount was $6.25 ÷ 25, or ? 

2. The pupils made colored Christmas bells with which to decorate the tree. If there were 100 bells when they were through, what was the average number of bells each child made? That is, how many bells did each child make, if each pupil made the same number?

The number of bells made by each child was 

100 ÷ 25, or ?

FOURTH GRADE 

3. One group of 5 pupils made wreaths with which to decorate the school. If they made 45 wreaths in all, what was the average number of wreaths each child made? 

4. A committee went down town to buy presents to put on the Christmas tree. They spent $1.56 and bought a present for each of the 25 children and for their teacher. What was the average cost of each present? 

5. One group of children made the sandwiches for the party. If they made 75 sandwiches for the 25 pupils, how many sandwiches did that average for each child? 

6. There were 275 nuts on the tree. How many nuts did that average for each child? 

7. Two girls made 26 candy bags for the children and their teacher. What was the average number each made? 

8. A committee of boys bought the candy and the popcorn for 26 bags. They spent $1.30.

What was the average cost of each bag? 

9. Six girls made green and red chains for decoration. If they made 90 yd. of chains, how many yards did each girl average? 

10. The children bought 15 Christmas balls for which they paid $1.20. What was the average price of each Christmas ball?

FINDING THE AVERAGE 

New Ability: To find the average when the total has to be found first. 

1. John made the following grades in arithmetic one week: Monday, 90; Tuesday, 85; Wednesday, 95; Thursday, 90; Friday, 100. He found his average for the week this way: He added the five * See Teaching Suggestions, Note 33 - Drill Device, page 385.

FOURTH GRADE 

numbers 90, 85, 95, 90, and 100. What was the sum? Then he divided this sum by 5. What quotient did he get? This number showed what his grade would have been each day if he had made the same grade every day. He said, "That's a pretty good average, even if I did do poorly on Tuesday." 

2. Jane's teacher averaged her three test grades in arithmetic to get Jane's grade for her report card. What was Jane's average if the three grades were 96, 95, and 94? 

3. One night Anne's father said, "It seems to me that our gas bills are rather large. I wonder how much they they average a month?" He found that the bills for the last 6 months were: 

August, $2.40; September, $2.85; October, $3.75; 

November, $7.25; December, $9.50; January, $10.25. What was the average monthly gas bill? 

4. Anne's father paid grocery bills for 4 months as follows: April, $45; May, $48; June, $51; 

July, $44. What was his average monthly grocery bill for the 4 months? 

5. On an automobile trip, John and his family traveled 225 mi. on Monday, 245 mi. on Tuesday, 275 mi. on Wednesday, 300 mi. on Thursday, and 320 mi. on Friday. What was the average number of miles they traveled each day?

6. One week Jim did odd jobs and earned money as follows: Monday, $.15; Tuesday, $.20; Wednesday, $.25; Thursday, $.10; Friday, $.25; Saturday, $.55. How much did he average a day? 

7. Jack got the following number of eggs one week from his flock of hens: Sunday, 12 eggs; 

Monday, 10 eggs; Tuesday, 14 eggs; Wednesday, 8 eggs; Thursday, 15 eggs; Friday, 11 eggs; and Saturday, 14 eggs. What was the average number of eggs laid each day?

LONG DIVISION 

New Ability: To solve and check long-division problems having two-digit quotients which end with a zero. 

Example 1. Divide 2820 by 47. 

When 282 is subtracted from 282, there is no remainder.

The next digit in the dividend to bring down is 0. As 0 ÷ 47 0, a zero is written in the quotient over the zero in the dividend. 60 47)2820 282 (Standard 1 in 2 minutes)* Divide: 1. 58)2320 

2. 20)1000 

3. 86)2580 

4. 54)4860 

5. 70)2100 

6. 41)3280

9. 33)2640 

10. 85)1700 

11. 87)3480 

12. 69)4140 * See Teaching Suggestions, Note 34 - Drill Device, page 385.

LONG DIVISION 

339 

Example 2. Divide 3445 by 49 and check the result. 

When 343 is subtracted from 344, the remainder is 1. Bringing down the 5 gives 15 for a partial dividend.

As 15 cannot be divided by 49, a zero is written in the quotient over the 5, and 15 is written as the remainder. 

To check a long-division problem with a remainder, multiply the quotient and the divisor together and add the remainder. If the result thus obtained is the same number as the dividend, the answer is correct.

In fractions, the number below the line, as 2 in 

1, 4 in 1, etc., gives the name of the fraction; it tells into how many equal parts a unit has been divided.

To express more than one of these equal parts, write the number of parts above the line. For example, "3 fourths" is written ; "4 fifths" is written ; "2 thirds "2 thirds" is written ; "5 

5 fourths " is written, etc.

PROBLEMS WITHOUT NUMBERS 

1. Your friend Mary comes to visit you on a certain date and tells you how many weeks she will stay. How can you tell the date on which she will leave? 

2. If you know how much a dealer paid for some hats and for how much he sold them, how can you find his gain or loss? 

3. If you know the number of days an automobile travels and the number of miles averaged each day, how can you find the total distance traveled? 

4. If there are the same number of sheep in each of a given number of pastures, how can you find the number of sheep in all the pastures?

7. If you know how many pints of milk each member of a family drinks each day and how many people there are in the family, how can you find the number of quarts of milk the family uses each day?

WORD MEANINGS 

361 

WORDS YOU USE IN ARITHMETIC 

angle: the corner formed by two straight lines area: the amount of surface enclosed by certain lines average: the answer you get when you divide the sum of several amounts by the number of the amounts in the problem decimal point: the period which separates dollars from cents fraction: one or more parts of a unit linear measure: a measure of length loss: the amount that is lost by selling something for less than it costs profit: the amount that is gained by selling something for more than it cost rectangle : a figure which has four straight sides and four square angles total: the answer to an addition problem
