TEACHING SUGGESTIONS FOR THE THIRD GRADE 

1. The primary addition facts must be made automatic. 

Pupils cannot do satisfactory work in addition until these facts are automatized; consequently much drill will be needed. Drill a few minutes every day. The 100 Primary

Addition Facts are given on page 19. The facts selected for this first group are limited to those whose sums do not exceed 9. 

2. Drill Device. Copy this wheel on the board and time the pupils individually to see if they can name all the an- 1 (+3) 2 swers correctly in 30 seconds. After each pupil has had a turn, give those who missed a second turn. Give those who failed the second round a third turn. After all who needed it have had three trials, change the number in the center and also those numbers on the rim which will make the sum exceed 9.

The pupils who succeed in the first round score 3 points, those who succeed in the second round score 2 points, those who succeed in the third round score 1 point. Individual scores may be kept through the period or the class may be divided into two teams and team scores kept. 2 3. In column addition the pupil should give only the 

In the first problem, adding up he should think "4, 9" or, adding down, "7, 9," and say "9." Each problem should be added both ways for two reasons: first, to start the checking habit; second, to give additional drill.

As no sum exceeds 9, these problems give drill on the easier addition facts, with the added difficulty of holding a sum in mind and adding it to the third number in the column. 2 375

THIRD GRADE 

4. Drill Device. Have the pupils hold the edge of a sheet of paper under the row of problems for which the answers are to be written. Name a row, give the signal to start, and let the pupils begin writing answers. The first pupil to finish the row indicated says "Stop!" The correct answers are read and each pupil scores a point (either for himself or for his team) for each correct answer. Do not check while using this device. 

5. The primary subtraction facts must be made automatic. Pupils cannot do satisfactory work in subtraction until these facts are automatized; consequently much drill is needed. Drill a few minutes every day. The 100 Primary

Subtraction Facts are given on pages 20 and 21. This list is taken from the easier facts, no minuend exceeding 9. 

6. Drill Device. The Drill Device in Note 2 is equally good for the primary subtraction facts. Use only eight numbers on the rim instead of ten. 

7. Drill Device. Use the Drill Device in Note 2; but let each pupil score if he can go completely around the wheel without making a mistake or let him score a point for each correct answer. Introduce timing when the class is ready for it, and work up to 8 correct answers in 30 seconds. 

8. These are the more difficult of the primary addition facts the facts whose sums exceed 9. If you have a group of pupils who have not had these facts developed in preceding grades, take a few facts at a time and have their sums found and the facts automatized. For drill, use the wheel device and the device in which the answers are written on paper. Flash cards may be profitably used for automatizing any of the primary facts, or the facts may be dictated rapidly by the teacher and the answers given by the pupils. Do not introduce the time element until there is sufficient mastery.

TEACHING SUGGESTIONS 

377 9. To check sums in addition, many teachers have the pupils first add the problem up, writing the answer over the problem. Then this sum is covered, the problem is added down, and the second sum is written under the problem. 

If the two sums are the same, as at the right, they are probably right. 79 32 47 79 Timed exercises should not be checked, as sufficient time is not allowed for working the problem twice; but all other problems should be checked. The habit of feeling dissatisfied until an answer has been proved correct should be well started in the third grade. Checking also gives additional practice. 

Pupils should get a great deal of practice (a few minutes at a time) in adding mentally three or more numbers set on a line, thus: Add 4, 9, 5, and 2. Such problems are called string problems." They may be dictated by the teacher. 

10. Drill Devices. (1) The teacher dictates a string problem and each pupil stands when he has the answer. Those who fail should do the problem again until all get the right answer. (2) A group of pupils may be sent to stand with their backs to the board while the problem is being dictated. 

After they get the answer, they turn and write it on the board. Each correct answer scores 1 point and the pupil who finishes first scores an additional point if his answer is correct. Team scores or individual scores may be kept. 

11. Drill Device. A good device for problems of this type is the one given in Note 4, except that a number of problems (three or four) should be given, instead of a whole row. 

12. Drill Device. Use the wheel device without timing.

A pupil may score for going completely around the wheel without missing or he may score 1 point for each correct answer. Use ten numbers on the rim.

THIRD GRADE 

13. Throughout the text the take-away method of subtraction is used for illustrative problems. This method was selected, not because it is the best, but because it is widely used. If you prefer the additive method or some other method, use it and substitute your explanation for the explanatory notes accompanying the problems illustrated. 

14. Each row of problems in this exercise presents a different zero situation. It is well to see that the first problem in each row can be solved by all the pupils before proceeding with the rest of the row. As large a group as possible may work at the board, while the rest of the pupils work at their seats. Dictate one problem at a time. 

Let all at the board who solved the problem correctly go back to their seats and other pupils take their places. 

This eliminates those needing no supervision and leaves at the board the pupils needing help. 

15. Drill Device. Put a wheel on the board with the multiplier in the center and the ten digits around the rim. 

Each pupil who can go around the wheel giving all the correct answers scores a point. 

16. These problems should be solved as multiplication problems. For example, in the first problem, 2 pencils will cost 2 times 5¢ (not 5¢ + 5¢). 

17. For the development of the idea of division, each pupil should be supplied with objects of some sort, such as toothpicks, beads, grains of corn, etc., which he can divide for himself. For this lesson each pupil needs 18 objects. 

18. Have the new facts found by using objects. Provide each pupil with the number required for the largest product. 

19. Have as large a group as possible work at the board, where the work can be closely supervised to see that wrong habits are not being set up. Alternate the groups.

TEACHING SUGGESTIONS 

379 

20. Have at least one of each coin, either in real money or in toy money, for the children to identify. The pupils can make toy money for playing store, making change, etc. 

21. Use toy money for this exercise. 

22. Drill Device. The wheel device gives good drill for this work. Put the divisor in the center and the dividends around the rim. (For the exercise on page 87, limit the dividends to numbers under 30.) 

23. Do not permit the pupils to use "and" in reading `these numbers. "And" is only properly used to separate an integer and a fraction in reading mixed numbers, either with decimals or common fractions. 66 24. Have the pupils find out by using objects that these 

Old Facts" are facts from other tables reversed. 

25. Drill Device. After the pupils have placed a sheet of paper beneath the row for which they are to write the answers, give the signal "Go." At the end of one minute say "Stop."

Check the answers, scoring 1 point for each correct answer, and give an additional point to those pupils who had as many as 4 answers right. Continue through the drill period. 

26. Drill on uneven divisions is a valuable preparation for long division. The wheel device is good for this drill, or you can use flash cards or give the problems to the pupils by turns, going around the class. 

27. Drill Device. Dictate one problem to be solved and checked at the seats. Give the signal to start, after explaining that the pupils are to number out loud as they finish writing their answers on their paper. The first one through says "One"; the second, "Two"; etc. More than one pupil may say the same number. When all are through, check the answers. Each pupil scores the number he called, if his answer is correct. If the answer is wrong, he adds 5

THIRD GRADE 

points to his score. Continue through the period. Total the scores at the end of the period. The lowest score wins. 

28. The 9's are introduced at this time, instead of in their natural order, so that there will be time for more drill on them than there would be if they were left for the end of the year. Probably the principal reason why the 9's are regarded as the most difficult of the multiplication facts is because they receive less drill on account of their late introduction. If it seems better, continue to find the products for each new fact by addition. 

29. At the end of the period, time the class to see how many can reach the standard for this type of problem. 

30. Time the class to see if they are up to the standard on these facts.

Diagnostic Test. This is a good test for finding out what facts each pupil hasn't sufficiently automatized. Have each pupil rule a sheet of paper into 90 squares - 10 rows with 9 squares in each. Dictate the combinations at the rate of 

3 seconds per combination (30 seconds per row). It will take considerable practice to get into the correct rhythm. 

The pupils write only the answers in the squares. All work in the same direction, either across or up and down. If a pupil has not started to write an answer when the next fact is given, he is to put a cross in the square and go on to the next problem. (See that the pupils do this; otherwise the test will not be diagnostic.) When finished, the papers may be exchanged and the answers may be read and checked. 

Each pupil can, by reference to his text, find the facts corresponding to his crosses, or those which he missed. These are the facts on which he needs additional practice. Use the Drill Device in Note 4, page 376. 

31. At least one thermometer is needed for teaching the measurement of temperature.

TEACHING SUGGESTIONS FOR THE FOURTH GRADE 

1. Drill Device. For mixed drill on additions in the upper decades, draw a wheel on the board and place numbers in the teens, twenties, and thirties around the rim of the wheel, with a single digit in the center. Let a pupil on one team go around, naming the sums. If he makes a mistake, he stops. If he names all the sums correctly, he scores a point for his team. A pupil on the opposite team then goes around, using the same number, but going in the opposite direction, scoring if he names all the sums correctly.

Change the number in the center and let another pair try to score. Continue throughout the drill period. 

2. When concrete problems are used for the purpose of building up the ability to analyze and plan solutions for problems, it is often well to stop with step 4. Problems without numbers, problems to be completed, and original problems furnish excellent drill in building up these abilities. 

3. Drill Device. Indicate a row for which the answers are to be written on a sheet of paper held under the row; then give the signal "Go." At the end of the time for a row of problems, say "Time"; but let the pupils who have not finished continue to work. When all have finished, read the answers. Score 1 point for each correct answer and 

1 additional point for finishing in time. 

4. Drill Device. Copy the wheel, changing the number in the center, and repeat the game. 

5. These problems involve the measurement idea of division. 

6. Start the pupils on the habit of checking in division. 

Check division by multiplying the quotient by the divisor to get the dividend. There are other check methods, but this is probably the best method for fourth-grade pupils. 

Timed problems are not to be checked. 381

FOURTH GRADE 7. Drill Device. For variation of the Drill Device in 

Note 3, the pupils may number out loud as they finish. 

When answers are checked, each pupil scores the number he called, adding a point for each wrong answer. with the smallest score wins. 

The pupil 8. Start the habit of checking subtraction problems. 

The best way is by addition, starting with the difference. 

Problems should not be checked when the work is timed. 

9. Drill Device. Send a group of pupils to the board, 43 51 35 344 with the rest of the class working at their seats. Give out any number from the first four decades. Each pupil writes the number at the top of his board space or, if he is working at his seat, at the top of his paper and draws 5, 3 r a line under it. Tell the class first to add, then to subtract, then to multiply, then to divide the number by a given digit, doing the work mentally. Thus, if the number given first is 43 and the digit to be used with it is 8, the pupils' answers should be arranged as shown above. 

Each pupil having all four results right scores 1 point; or 1 point may be allowed for each correct result. Change the numbers frequently. Give every pupil one or more chances to work at the board. All the work should be mental. Only the results should be written. 

10. Toward the end of the period, time the class to see how many can reach the standard. Never check when timing. A good drill device is given in Note 3. 

11. The words less and left cause difficulty, as they are usually subtraction cues. Rationalize these problems carefully. Make the situations real. Have the pupils make problems of their own and solve them. 

12. Drill Device. Repeat the Drill Device in Note 9 above, using any two-digit numbers.

TEACHING SUGGESTIONS 

383 13. Do not permit fourth-grade pupils to hand in problems with incorrect answers. Emphasize checking, except when timing to see if the class is up to standard. These standards do not include time for checking. The best check for addition is to add in the opposite direction. At the end of the period, time the class. 

14. This lesson may be developed inductively. 

15. These problems should be solved on the board or under close supervision at the seats, to see that the right habits are started in the new process of long multiplication. 

16. While the checking habit is being built up, it is well sometimes to let the pupils check their own answers after a timed exercise instead of having the answers read to them. 

17. Actual weighing of articles which are bought by weight should be done by the children. If large scales are available, the children should weigh one another so that they may learn to read the scales. 

18. Making related problems from a given problem helps to make situations real to the pupils. 

19. Let some pupils make up problems of their own and other pupils make the related problems. 

20. Start the habit of checking long multiplication. The most practical method is to do the problem twice. 

21. Time the class. Score a point for each correct answer. 

Check at the end of the timing. 

22. Actual measuring should be done by the pupils, using standard measures. 

23. The time element should not be emphasized until the long-multiplication process is well fixed. The checking for correct answers must also be made a habit, but the time for this cannot be included in a standard. When timing is done, certain indicated problems will have to be copied before starting to time the solutions.

FOURTH GRADE 

24. Drill Device. Let pupils from each of two teams go to the board. The rest of the class work at their seats. 

Dictate a problem and have the pupils stand with their backs to the board until the signal is given to start. When "Time" is called, let the pupils who have finished raise their hands. Let the slower pupils keep on working until the problem is finished. Have each pupil start checking his problem as soon as he has finished it. Each pupil with a correct answer scores 1 point. Each pupil who had the correct answer when time was called scores an additional point. 

Send another group to the board and repeat. This device can be used for the whole class when working at their seats. 

25. If, after working with this group of problems, the pupils still find it awkward to write the quotient over the dividend, turn back to other lists of short-division problems. 

26. Drill Device. For drill in holding numbers in mind and adding dictated numbers, these problems may be dictated as "string problems." They should be dictated slowly enough so that each addition can be made before the next addend is given. At the end of the period, time the class. 

27. Procure pint and quart milk bottles with which the pupils can do actual measuring. 

28. Keep the pupils on problems with one-digit divisors, with the work written out, until the steps are automatized. 

Go back often to other similar lists of division problems. 

29. Drill Device. Place these numbers in a horizontal line on the board: 40, 70, 10, 60, 20, 90, 30, 50, 80.

Let each pupil take a turn at multiplying the nine numbers through by a number that is given him. For example, if the number 7 is given a pupil, he should stand and recite the following products: 280, 490, 70, 420, 140, 630, 210, 350, and 560. If he makes no mistakes, he scores 1 point for his team. Change the multiplier each time. Let the pupils

TEACHING SUGGESTIONS 

385 start at the right-hand side part of the time. This is valuable drill preparatory to long division. 

30. Have toy money made to be used with this lesson. 

31. Mastery of this type of problem will be very valuable in preparation for accurate estimation of partial quotients in the long-division problems, which are to follow shortly. 

32. Drill Device. Send as many pupils as possible to the board and dictate the problems to be solved. Score 2 points for each correct answer and 1 additional point for getting through first, if the answer is correct. The pupils at their seats should also solve every problem. Change the groups at the board frequently. 

33. Drill Device. Dictate one problem to the class and give the signal "Go." The pupil who finishes first should call out "One" and write this number on his paper, and so on. If several finish at the same time, they use the same number. All who get the correct answer score the number they called out. Those who get wrong answers add 5 points to their score. The lowest score is the best. 

34. Drill Device. Dictate a problem and give the signal "Go." At the end of the time for one problem, say "Time"; but have the pupils who have not finished continue to work. When all have finished, read the answer. 

Score 1 point for the correct answer and 1 additional point for the correct answer finished within the time. 

35. Do not introduce the time element until the longdivision process is fairly well automatized. Supervise the work closely to see that all the pupils are working correctly. 

36. All work with fractions should be oral. 

37. Drill Device. Use the Drill Device in Note 3, page 

381, except that 2 to 4 problems should be assigned, instead of a whole row. Or use the Drill Device in Note 34 above. 

38. These problems involve the partition idea of division.
